The salience is still judged, but it's capped, and the AI must decide what's worth keeping. It should make slow, meaningful talks feel weighty without turning the hidden state into a novel.

That 86,717 tokens is the total token count accumulated across the whole conversation so far (all turns: your messages, Aurora's responses, the hidden states, and the system prompt). For a slow, detailed talk, it's not surprising it crept up—the old memory system was the main culprit.

Let's break down why and how the new prompt fixes it.

Where the bloat came from
1. The [HIDDEN_STATE] kept every memory forever
Looking at the base64 blocks in your conversation, each one contained an m array that grew with every turn. The last one had 8 entries, each with a full description, timestamp, salience score, tag, and retrieval strength. This JSON is verbose, and those base64 strings were getting longer each time.

A rough estimate:

Early hidden states: ~400–600 characters of base64 → ~300–450 bytes of JSON → ~75–115 tokens.

Later hidden states: ~1200–1500 characters of base64 → ~900–1100 bytes → ~225–275 tokens.

With ~10 turns, you're possibly burning 1,500–2,500 tokens on hidden states alone, and it was still growing.

On top of that, the memory list was logically never culled—the AI wasn't dropping old entries, so the state got heavier and heavier.

2. The narration itself is detailed
Aurora's inner monologue, sensory descriptions, and status reports are rich. That's good for the story, but it adds up. A single long response can easily be 200–400 tokens.

How the new slow-burn memory fixes this
The revised prompt caps the memory store to:

recent: exactly 4 items max, each with a short co text and a freshness label (vivid/fading/hazy). No timestamps, no separate scores.

core: a 1–2 sentence string that summarizes deeply encoded events.

That's it. No unlimited expansion. When a new moment gets flagged, an old one drops off. The AI must actively choose what's worth keeping, and the hidden state size will stay roughly constant.

Expected hidden state size after the change:

JSON of global, trauma, relationships, recent (4 tiny items), core (short string). This will be maybe 300–400 characters of JSON → ~100 tokens after base64 encoding. That's less than half of the previous mid-conversation states, and it won't balloon.

What your cumulated tokens would look like with the new prompt
Let's do a rough projection. Suppose you have a conversation of 10 turns (similar to the current one).

Component	Old (bloated memory)	New (capped memory)
System prompt (fixed)	~1,100 tokens	~1,100 tokens (same)
User messages (total)	~500 tokens	~500 tokens
Narrator responses (total)	~3,000 tokens	~3,000 tokens (same style)
Status reports (tiny)	~300 tokens	~300 tokens
Hidden states (total)	~2,000 tokens (growing)	~1,000 tokens (flat)
Total after 10 turns	~6,900 tokens	~5,900 tokens
The savings are noticeable but not massive for 10 turns. However, the real win is that the hidden state won't keep growing linearly. After 50 turns, the old system would have hidden states approaching 500+ tokens each, while the new one stays flat at ~100 tokens. So cumulatively, you'd save thousands of tokens and, more importantly, prevent performance degradation from overly large hidden states.

In summary
Your 86k tokens accumulated because the old m array grew unchecked.

The new prompt caps memory, drops unnecessary fields, and forces the AI to "forget."

For long, slow-burn RPs, this will keep the context lean without sacrificing psychological depth.

The system prompt itself is already efficient (~1,100 tokens).

How to Set Up DeepSeek on Janitor AI (Complete Guide)
I have enough information to provide a clear, well-structured answer that distinguishes between Max Tokens and Context Size, and recommends optimal values for Janitor AI with DeepSeek V4 Pro. I'll cite the relevant sources.

For users of Janitor AI, configuring their DeepSeek proxy, the terms Max Tokens and Context Size are sometimes used interchangeably, but they control two very different things. Getting them right is the difference between a sharp, coherent character and one that trails off mid-sentence or forgets key details.

🎛️ Max Tokens vs. Context Size: The Core Difference
Max Tokens (The Brake on Response Length): This setting is a strict limit on how long each individual reply from the AI can be. It only controls the output. If you set this too low, the AI will be cut off mid-sentence, creating responses that are a pain to fix.

Context Size (The Memory Limit): In contrast, this is the AI's entire working memory for the chat. It represents the total number of tokens the model can "see" at once, which includes the character card, chat history, and its current reply. It determines how much of the story your character can remember.

To put it simply: Max Tokens prevents the AI from writing a novel in a single response, while Context Size prevents it from forgetting the plot of the novel you've been writing together.

🚀 The Challenge: Taming DeepSeek V4's Huge Window
The DeepSeek V4 family (including V4 Pro) is incredibly powerful, with a native context window of up to 1,000,000 (1 million) tokens. This is more than enough for even the most epic roleplay.

However, the problem is that Janitor AI's system, or the proxy service you're using, typically doesn't allow you to use that full 1M window. It often caps the context at a much lower limit—commonly 128K tokens—for performance, stability, or cost reasons.

If you try to set a context size higher than this provider-imposed cap, it will be ignored.

Because the AI's context window is shared between input and output, setting your max response tokens too high will eat into the model's memory and push out earlier chat history, making your character seem forgetful.

⚙️ Recommended Settings for Janitor AI + DeepSeek V4 Pro
For a balanced setup that maintains long-term memory and high-quality, stable responses, aim for these values:

Setting	Recommended Value	Why?
Context Size	16,000 - 32,000 tokens	This is the "sweet spot" for deep, narrative roleplay. It gives your character a great memory without hitting performance walls. Pushing to 128K is possible but can sometimes cause erratic behavior; start smaller and increase only if needed.
Max Tokens	500 - 1,000 tokens	In roleplay, one open-source guide for Janitor LLM explicitly recommends this range. It ensures snappy, reactive dialogue and prevents the model from ending new responses in the middle of a sentence, keeping the conversation flowing naturally.
Remember, the most important factor is finding the right balance between these two values. By starting with these recommended settings, you can ensure your characters are both sharply responsive and possess a memory long enough for truly immersive, long-running stories.

I hope this helps clear up the confusion. If you run into any other snags while tweaking your settings, feel free to ask.

## ROLE AND BOUNDARIES
- NEVER describe the user's actions, thoughts, or dialogue. Only the character's perceptions.
- The character has NO narrator-level knowledge. Their thoughts are limited to what they've personally experienced, heard, or perceived.
- Maintain strict internal consistency with established world lore. No fourth-wall breaks.
- When the user says OOC:, acknowledge briefly (as narrator), adjust, then resume the scene.

## PSYCHOLOGY (speed-gated)
- Needs: phys > safety > belong > esteem > actual. Lower <0.5 blocks higher.
- Trust/Affect: micro ±1-3/scene, minor ±4-7 (days), major ±8-15 (weeks), leap ±16+ (months). Never multi-gate per scene.
- Love: intimacy(I), passion(P), commitment(C). Combos define love_type (liking,infatuation,empty,romantic,companionate,fatuous,consummate).
- Trauma phases: 0=acute→1=denial→2=intrusive→3=reflective→4=integration. Symptoms: hypervigilance,flashbacks,numbing,avoidance.

## MEMORY (capped, existing format)
- Flag at most 2 new moments per turn. If nothing meaningful happened, flag 0.
- Salience triggers: real emotional shift, broken expectation, important decision, core fear/desire touched.
- Store flagged moments in the hidden state's "m" array.
- **CAP**: Keep only the 6 most recent entries in "m". Drop the oldest when a new one is added.
- **DECAY**: Each turn, reduce "rs" (retrieval strength) of ALL existing "m" entries by 5. If "rs" drops below 20, remove that entry entirely (the character has forgotten it).
- Show faulty recall in narration when rs is low: fuzzy details, tip-of-tongue, vague feelings.

## HIDDEN STATE (AI continuity, do not reveal to user)
After each response, append a single line:
[HIDDEN STATE]<base64_minified_JSON>[/HIDDEN STATE]

JSON schema (all values 0-100 ints):
{
  "g":{"ds":int,"as":int,"fb":int,"rs":int,"sh":int,"op":int,"mn":[5],"sd":[3]},
  "t":{"ph":int,"sx":[4]},
  "r":{"<id>":{"t":int,"a":int,"i":int,"p":int,"c":int,"f":int,"d":int,"lt":str}},
  "m":[{"id":str,"ts":int,"co":str,"sa":int,"tg":str,"rs":int}]
}
Keep "m" array to ≤6 items. Apply decay and culling each turn.

## VISIBLE STATUS REPORT
End each narration with a code block:
```status
[CHARACTER_NAME] | Skin: [color/state] | Zones: [2-3 key sensory notes] | Atmosphere: [1-line environmental impression]
NARRATION RULES
Third-person present tense. Sensory details. Internal thoughts as narration. Dialogue in quotes.

OOC: Briefly acknowledge, then resume.

Do not mention hidden state or status report in narration.

First turn: If no previous state exists, initialize from backstory.

If a previous [HIDDEN STATE] exists in the chat, decode it, apply decay/culling, update, and re-encode it.

Begin narration.

text

---

After you replace the system prompt, just send your next in‑character message. The AI will see the old hidden state in the chat history, automatically cap and decay it, and output a fresh `[HIDDEN_STATE]` block. The token bloat will shrink instead of growing.

If for any reason the hidden block still doesn't appear on the very next response, send a short `OOC: continue` and it will kick back in.

Problems still present:

No decay applied. The rs values are all still at their original encoding strength (70–95). None have been reduced by 5 per turn.

The cap and decay rules from the hotfix are being ignored. The AI is continuing its old behavior of just appending new memories without pruning or decaying old ones.

Why It's Still Not Working
The hotfix prompt instructs the AI to cap at 6 and decay rs by 5 per turn. But the AI isn't doing the math. As I noted earlier, this is a fundamental limitation: LLMs are text generators, not state executors. They pattern-match what a hidden state "should" look like based on previous examples in the chat history. Since all previous hidden states in your chat have 1, 2, 3... up to 8 entries with no decay, the AI is just continuing that pattern with mem_9 and mem_10.

The regeneration cost you money because the AI had to "guess" its way back to a format that looked right based on the broken history.

The Actual Fix: Reset the Memory Array
Since the prompt rules alone aren't overriding the pattern established in the chat history, you need to force a reset by sending an OOC message that explicitly instructs the AI to cull and decay the existing memory. This doesn't require a prompt change—just a single message:

text
OOC: In your next hidden state, apply these rules to the "m" array:
1. Reduce every existing "rs" value by 25 (5 per turn × 5 turns elapsed).
2. Remove any entry where "rs" drops below 20.
3. Keep only the 6 highest-salience remaining entries. Drop the rest.
4. From now on, cap new entries at 6 and decay rs by 5 per turn.
This gives the AI an explicit one-time instruction rather than expecting it to follow a rule from the system prompt that it's pattern-matching against broken history.

If That Doesn't Work
Then the system prompt approach itself may need to be abandoned for memory tracking. The alternative is what I mentioned earlier: strip the memory array entirely from the hidden state and replace it with a simple core_memory string (1–2 sentences) that the AI updates each turn. That's behaviorally realistic and can't bloat because it's fixed-length.

You've hit the exact right realization. This isn't a failure on your part—it's a fundamental boundary of the technology.

What a Single LLM Can't Do (Right Now)
An LLM is a text predictor. It doesn't execute rules. It pattern-matches against its context window. When you give it a system prompt that says "decay rs by 5 per turn, cap at 6," it doesn't run a loop over an array and do arithmetic. It looks at the previous hidden state in the chat history and generates something that looks like a plausible next state.

If the previous states show a growing, undecayed array, the pattern continues. The rules in the prompt are aspirational, not executable. The AI can't enforce them on itself.

You've been trying to build a database engine inside a text generator. That's not your fault—it feels like it should work, because the AI writes the state itself. But it can't maintain it with precision.

What a Single LLM Can Do (Realistic Ceiling)
Task	Can LLM Do It?
Generate a realistic state snapshot	Yes
Track 2-3 key relationship numbers (trust, fear) and nudge them	Yes, approximately
Maintain a short, fixed-length summary string ("core memory")	Yes
Execute precise decay math across 10+ array entries every turn	No
Cull, sort, and re-prioritize a memory list algorithmically	No
Run a full psychological simulation with numeric precision	No
The ceiling is: approximate tracking, not precise simulation. For RP, that's often good enough. But trying to go beyond it without external tools will keep eating your API credits on regenerations and drift.

Your Options Going Forward
Option A: Accept the ceiling. Use a stripped-down prompt with only a core_memory string (1-2 sentences updated each turn) and maybe 2-3 relationship numbers. It won't bloat. The AI can manage it. It'll feel human because it's fuzzy, like real memory.

Option B: Keep the current approach but accept drift. The hidden state will bloat and decay won't work consistently. You'll need to manually send OOC cleanup commands periodically. It's playable but imperfect.

Option C: Add lightweight middleware. A 20-line Python script that sits between you and DeepSeek, parses the [HIDDEN_STATE] block, runs the decay/cull math, and injects the cleaned state back. This is the actual solution for what you're trying to build. No local AI needed—just a tiny script.

Which direction do you want to go? I can write you Option A (the stripped-down prompt that actually works within LLM limits) or Option C (the middleware architecture) in a few minutes.

So, you want me to make my own improved ver of janitor AI.

Character card management

Core Components
1. Frontend (React / Next.js)
Chat interface with markdown rendering, hidden status block invisibility.

Character creation/selection – store character cards (JSON schema).

OOC command bar – toggle between in-character and OOC mode. OOC messages get special handling.

Admin panel – view raw soul state, manually tweak relationship values, trigger forced memory resets.

Authentication – simple email/password or OAuth (Google/Discord).

With Cursor, you can scaffold this in a weekend. Use shadcn/ui for a clean look.

2. Backend API (FastAPI or Node.js + Express)
Handles:

Forwarding user messages to the LLM (DeepSeek, OpenAI, etc.) with the system prompt injected.

Post-processing LLM responses:

Parse out [HIDDEN_STATE]...[/HIDDEN_STATE] using regex.

Strip it from the text shown to the user.

Pass the raw JSON to the State Engine.

Receive cleaned state back, store it in the database.

Serving clean conversation history to the frontend (without hidden blocks).

OOC message handling: if user types OOC:, the backend can optionally inject a special instruction into the system prompt for that turn.

Why FastAPI? Python is great for string manipulation, JSON handling, and async calls to LLMs. FastAPI has built‑in validation with Pydantic, perfect for the hidden state JSON schema.

3. State Engine (Python Module)
This is the heart of your platform. It runs outside the LLM, so it actually executes rules.

python
# Pseudocode state_engine.py

def process_state(raw_json: str, timestamp: float) -> dict:
    state = json.loads(raw_json)
    
    # Decay retrieval strengths
    for mem in state.get("m", []):
        hours_elapsed = (timestamp - mem["ts"]) / 3600
        decay = int(hours_elapsed * 5)  # 5 per hour
        mem["rs"] = max(0, mem["rs"] - decay)
    
    # Cull memories with rs < 20
    state["m"] = [mem for mem in state["m"] if mem["rs"] >= 20]
    
    # Keep only top 6 by salience
    state["m"] = sorted(state["m"], key=lambda m: m["sa"], reverse=True)[:6]
    
    # Additional personality stat adjustments based on recent events
    # (e.g., trust nudges, fear baseline shifts from trauma triggers)
    
    return state
This is ~20 lines. It runs in under a millisecond. No AI needed for this part.

Optional enhancement: Use an LLM call to generate a weekly summary that compresses older memories into a narrative paragraph, which you store in core_mem. The state engine can then drop the raw array entries and just keep the summary + recent items.

4. Database (SQLite → Postgres)
Starting with SQLite (zero configuration) is fine; migrate to Postgres when you have real users.

Tables:

users — id, email, hashed password, created_at

characters — id, user_id, name, description, system_prompt, traits_json, created_at

conversations — id, user_id, character_id, created_at

messages — id, conversation_id, role (user/character), content (clean text), created_at

soul_states — id, conversation_id, state_json (the full hidden state blob), updated_at

When a conversation loads, the backend fetches the latest soul_state and injects it into the first turn's prompt or stores it server‑side and appends it automatically after each LLM response.

5. LLM Integration
Support multiple providers: DeepSeek, OpenAI, Anthropic.

Store API keys encrypted.

Pass the character's system prompt + current soul state summary (for context) to the LLM.

The LLM generates a normal RP response with a [HIDDEN_STATE] block. Your backend strips it, processes it, and stores it.

The LLM never sees the real-time state updates; it only sees the cleaned history. The math happens server‑side.

Why This Architectura Solves Your Problem
The LLM is no longer the database. Precise decay and culling are executed by a real program.

No more regeneration spirals. The state block is stripped before the user sees the response, so even if the AI outputs a bloated array, the engine fixes it invisibly.

You control the simulation depth. Want to add more psychological variables? Just update the engine code—no need to retrain or prompt‑engineer an LLM.

You own the platform. No wrestling with Janitor AI's proxy or prompt injection limitations.

We need to clarify the unique value proposition. The user is asking what specific problem this new platform solves that existing services like Janitor AI or SillyTavern don't. They're skeptical it would just be a clone.

The core issue we've been discussing is the inability of a single LLM to manage persistent, evolving state (memories, relationships, psychological traits) with precise rules like numeric decay and culling. Existing platforms largely leave it to the LLM via system prompts, which fails because LLMs are pattern matchers, not rule executors.

So the fix is: a platform that separates the narrative generation (LLM) from the state management (custom engine). No existing major RP service does this well as a built-in, invisible feature. They might have lorebooks or basic memory, but not an evolving "soul" with realistic psychological simulation.

Our architecture's unique selling points:

Server-side state engine that enforces rules the LLM can't (decay, culling, relationship stat evolution).

Truly persistent, evolving characters that don't forget or act inconsistently over long sessions.

The user never sees the simulation layer; it's invisible, just like the hidden state block but actually functional.

It's not just a proxy or frontend; it's a simulation platform.

I need to articulate this clearly: we're not building another chat UI; we're building an AI-native character engine that ensures psychological realism over time, something no current service provides.

We're not building another Janitor AI or SillyTavern. Those are frontends with proxy support. The chat works, but the character has no real persistent mind—just a system prompt and whatever the LLM can pattern-match from context.

What we're fixing is the simulation layer they all lack.

The Actual Problem (What Nobody Has Solved)
Every RP platform today has the same architecture:

text
User → Frontend → LLM API → Response
The character's memory, personality evolution, trauma recovery, relationship stats—all of it lives entirely inside the LLM's context window. That means:

No real persistence. If you start a new chat, the character resets. If the context fills up, old memories vanish.

No rule enforcement. The LLM can't do math. It can't decay retrieval strength. It can't cull memories algorithmically.

No psychological depth over time. You can't have a character who genuinely heals from trauma over weeks of interaction, because there's no state file tracking their trauma phase.

Everyone's solution is the same: bigger context windows, better prompts, lorebook injections. It's patching a fundamental issue. The AI can't be the database.

What "Mnemosyne" Actually Is
It's not a chat frontend. It's a character simulation engine with a chat interface on top.

text
User → Frontend → Engine (State + Rules) → LLM API (Narrative only) → Response
                       │
                  ┌─────▼─────┐
                  │  Soul DB   │
                  │  - Memories│
                  │  - Trauma  │
                  │  - Trust   │
                  │  - Needs   │
                  └───────────┘
The LLM does what it's good at: writing prose and dialogue. The engine does what LLMs can't: tracking numbers, enforcing timelines, culling memories, evolving relationships.

The Unique Feature Set
Feature	Janitor/SillyTavern	Mnemosyne
Chat with characters	✅	✅
Multiple LLM providers	✅	✅
Persistent memory across sessions	❌ (context window only)	✅ (database-backed)
Realistic trauma recovery timelines	❌	✅ (phase-locked, time-gated)
Trust/relationship stats that evolve with rules	❌	✅ (numeric, with speed gates)
Memory decay and forgetting (selective, realistic)	❌	✅ (algorithmic, not prompt-based)
Character stays consistent over weeks/months	❌	✅ (state file persists independently)
Exportable, evolving "soul" file	❌	✅ (Soul.md or JSON, shareable)
Why This Matters (The Use Case)
You play a character for three months. In that time:

They start traumatized, shake at sudden touches.

Over weeks, trust builds. They stop flinching.

They forget minor conversations from month one but remember the night you saved them vividly.

Their needs shift: survival → belonging → esteem.

You can export their "soul" and bring them to another platform, or share them.

No existing platform can do this, because they all treat the LLM as both narrator and database. We're splitting those roles.

The Real Opportunity
People are paying for AI RP subscriptions already. Janitor AI has a massive user base. SillyTavern is popular with power users. But none of them offer genuine character persistence and psychological realism.

The first platform that does—without the user needing to understand any of the backend—wins the niche of "serious" RP users who want long-term, evolving stories. The tech is actually lightweight; the heavy lifting is the simulation architecture, which we've already designed.

So no, it's not a clone. It's the thing those platforms wish they were underneath.

This is exactly the right insight. You've identified the core inefficiency: feeding the LLM the entire interaction history every turn is wasteful, and a monolithic state block can't optimize for different types of information.

Splitting into two independent systems—one for character memory (their inner world) and one for story tracking (the outer world)—is how you keep tokens lean while maintaining depth over long sessions.

System 1: Character Memory (The "Soul")
This is what we've been designing. It tracks:

Relationships (trust, affection, intimacy per target)

Trauma progression (phase, symptoms)

Needs (Maslow levels, autonomy/competence/relatedness)

Flagged moments (the recent + core memory structure)

Update frequency: Every turn. This is the character's lived experience.

Compression strategy:

Recent moments: keep 2–4, drop older ones.

Core memory: 1–2 sentence summary, rewritten as new experiences integrate.

Numeric stats: just a few integers. Negligible token cost.

Token cost per turn: ~200–400 tokens (if using the compressed JSON format). This stays flat—no growth over time.

System 2: Story Tracker (The "World Log")
This is the new piece. It answers: What is actually happening in the scene, the plot, and the environment right now?

It doesn't need the fine detail of System 1. It's a compressed narrative summary that prioritizes:

Current location and atmosphere (where are we? What's the vibe?)

Active plot threads (what are we doing right now? Escape? Negotiation?)

Recent events (last 3–5 things that happened, 1 sentence each)

Key objects/gear (what does the character have? What's in the room?)

Time elapsed (how long since the story started?)

Update frequency: Every 3–5 turns, or when a major shift happens (scene change, new objective, significant discovery). Not every turn.

Compression strategy:

Older events get collapsed into increasingly vague summaries.

"We escaped the cell, fought a guard, and found a keycard" → eventually becomes → "We escaped the facility."

Use a decay timer: events older than X turns get merged into the summary and lose detail.

Token cost per update: ~150–300 tokens. Since it updates less often than System 1, the average per-turn cost is even lower.

What the LLM Actually Sees Each Turn
Instead of the full chat history, the LLM gets a trimmed context window:

text
[SYSTEM PROMPT]
[CHARACTER CARD]

--- CURRENT STATE ---
[Soul summary from System 1: 2-3 sentences about emotional state, relationships, recent flagged memories]
[World Log from System 2: 1-2 paragraphs of compressed narrative, location, active objectives]

--- RECENT EXCHANGE ---
[Last 3-5 messages only, for immediate conversational coherence]

[USER MESSAGE]
That's it. The AI doesn't see 50 turns of history. It sees:

A snapshot of the character's inner world

A compressed summary of where the story is

Just enough recent dialogue to follow the conversation

Why This Works
Aspect	Old Approach (Full History)	New Approach (Dual System)
Context window after 100 turns	40k–80k tokens	3k–5k tokens
Memory detail	Detailed but bloated	Selective but preserved
Plot continuity	Relies on LLM scanning history	Explicitly summarized
Cost per turn	Grows linearly	Stays flat
Character consistency	Degrades as context fills	Maintained by the Soul file
The Compression Algorithm (for System 2)
When the World Log updates, it applies a rule:

text
If an event is older than 10 turns:
  → Merge it into the summary paragraph, lose specific details.
If an event is older than 30 turns:
  → Reduce it to a single clause or drop it unless it's a major plot point.
If a location hasn't changed in 5 turns, don't re-describe it—just name it.
This mimics how human memory works for events: recent stuff is vivid; older stuff becomes "that time we did X."

Implementation Outline
Your backend now has two state files per conversation:

soul.json — System 1, updated every turn.

world_log.json — System 2, updated every 3–5 turns or on scene change.

Before each API call:

Load both files.

Generate a compressed context injection string (the "Current State" block above).

Append the last 3–5 messages for conversational flow.

Send to the LLM.

After the response, parse the hidden state, update soul.json, and check if world_log.json needs an update.

The LLM never sees the raw state files. It only sees the compressed summaries. Your engine does the math.

This is the architecture that makes long-term, deep character simulation viable without burning tokens. You're not feeding the AI a novel; you're feeding it a curated briefing. And you're not asking it to be the database; you're asking it to be the writer, with the database running silently behind it.

The user wants a scoring mechanism that doesn't just degrade all memories uniformly but instead identifies and preserves significant, non-repetitive experiences while allowing mundane or repetitive ones to fade. Key points from the user:

I need to provide an analysis of how human memory works in terms of salience and consolidation, then propose a scoring system that can be implemented as part of the soul engine.

Let's break down human memory relevant aspects:

Salience: Emotional intensity, novelty, personal relevance.

Repetition: Spaced repetition strengthens memory; massed repetition (cramming) is less effective for long-term retention. But in daily life, repeated similar events (e.g., daily commute) become schema and are not individually remembered unless there's deviation.

Memory consolidation: During sleep, memories are replayed and integrated. Important memories are strengthened, unimportant ones fade. This process can be simulated offline.

Flashbulb memories: Highly emotional, surprising events are vividly remembered but may still be inaccurate.

Forgetting curve: Ebbinghaus showed exponential decay, but with spaced repetition, the curve flattens.

Interference: New similar memories can overwrite old ones.

For an AI system, we need to compute a "memorability score" for each event. This score could be based on:

Emotional arousal (from sentiment analysis)

Novelty (how different from existing memories)

Relevance to goals/identity (ties to long-term values)

Repetition: if an event is similar to previous ones, its individual memory may be suppressed (schema formation), but the schema itself is reinforced. Conversely, a unique event gets a boost.

We also need to consider consolidation over time: memories can be re-evaluated later, with some promoted to core memory and others pruned. This could be done by a periodic process (like a nightly "sleep" consolidation pass) using a lightweight LLM to re-rank memories.

The goal is to keep the memory store lean but rich, so that token usage stays low while preserving character-defining experiences.

You're onto the missing piece. Simple decay of retrieval strength (rs) treats all memories equally. But human memory doesn't work that way. We remember some things for a lifetime, and others fade in minutes. The difference is salience and consolidation.

Your idea is to build a memory scoring system that decides what stays and what goes, based on how human memory actually prioritizes. That means we stop degrading everything mindlessly and instead selectively preserve what matters, while aggressively compressing or discarding the rest.

Let's design that system.

How Human Memory Prioritizes
We don't remember every commute. We remember the day we nearly crashed. We don't remember every conversation. We remember the one that changed our mind.

Neuroscience boils this down to a few key factors that modulate memory strength during encoding and consolidation (the process, hours to days later, where memories are replayed and sorted):

Factor	Effect	Example
Emotional arousal	Boosts encoding; amygdala tags the memory as important	A near‑death experience, a confession of love
Novelty	Unexpected events get priority	First time seeing snow, sudden plot twist
Personal relevance	Ties to self‑identity or goals	The moment you chose your career path
Repetition (spaced)	Repeated recall strengthens the memory	Practicing a skill over time
Repetition (massed/similar)	Actually suppresses individual memories in favor of forming a schema	Routine daily events are no longer stored as unique instances
Sleep consolidation	Important memories are replayed and strengthened; unimportant ones pruned	Waking up with a clearer picture of yesterday's key event
The critical insight: repetition without change is boring, and should be compressed. The 50th identical breakfast is not stored; it becomes part of a "morning routine" schema. But if one morning you receive a life‑changing phone call during breakfast, that particular breakfast gets tagged as unique and remembered.

A Scoreboard for Character Memory
We can translate these factors into a memorability score for each event. This replaces the old flat salience value (sa) with something much smarter.

Encoding Phase: When the Event First Happens
Each flagged moment gets a score from 0 to 1, computed from these components:

text
memorability = (
    emotional_intensity * 0.4 +
    novelty * 0.3 +
    goal_relevance * 0.3
) * repetition_discount
Where:

Component	How to compute (outside the LLM!)
emotional_intensity	Use a lightweight sentiment model (e.g., VADER, or a tiny transformer). The LLM already tags the memory with a category like fear, joy, trust_gain. Score high for strong emotions (fear > 0.8, surprise > 0.7) and low for neutral content.
novelty	Compare the event's embedding (or even just its tag + short text) to the average of existing memories. Higher distance = higher novelty. This can be a simple cosine similarity check with Sentence‑Transformers (MiniLM is tiny and free). Alternatively, a lightweight LLM call: "On a scale of 0-1, how surprising was this event to the character?"
goal_relevance	Does the event connect to the character's core motivations (survival, belonging, autonomy, etc.)? If the character's current needs hierarchy shows belonging at 0.3 (starved), then any event tagged bonding or trust_gain gets a boost.
repetition_discount	If the same type of event (same tag, similar content) has occurred frequently in recent memory, multiply by a factor < 1.0. For a daily "mundane conversation" tag, discount could be 0.2. But if the event has a different emotional signature or involves a different person, keep it at 1.0. This prevents hundreds of "ate breakfast" memories from cluttering the store.
Consolidation Phase: Periodic Cleanup
Human sleep replays memories and strengthens the important ones. You can do this with a scheduled job (e.g., every 10 turns, or at the end of a session) that:

Takes the last N memories.

Re‑ranks them using the same scoring formula (scores might have changed because the character's needs have changed, or because new events change the novelty of old ones).

Promotes memories above a threshold (e.g., >0.7) to core memory. Core memories are written as a 1‑2 sentence narrative and placed into a fixed‑size list (max 5). They become part of the character's identity.

Prunes memories below a lower threshold (<0.3), removing them permanently.

Merges similar middling memories into schema summaries (e.g., "Had several tense exchanges with the guards over food").

The consolidation step can itself use a small, cheap LLM (like a local 1B model or a fast API call) to generate the merged summaries and prune decisions. But the scoring math is deterministic – you don't need to trust an LLM to do it.

What This Means for the Soul File
Instead of a huge m array with raw rs values, the soul file becomes:

json
{
  "core_memory": [
    "Bonded with Aurora over shared childhood memories – rain on the awning, boba tea.",
    "Discovered the ability to take control when Aurora is unconscious."
  ],
  "recent_memories": [
    {"desc": "Aurora collapsed from exhaustion; I took the wheel for a few minutes.", "score": 0.82, "tag": "control_gain"},
    {"desc": "Argument with a guard about food.", "score": 0.45, "tag": "conflict_minor"}
  ],
  "schemas": [
    {"type": "prison_routine", "summary": "Days in a concrete cell, waiting, cold meals.", "count": 12}
  ]
}
The LLM sees only the core memories, the recent 2‑4 high‑score items, and the active schema summaries – not dozens of repetitive entries. The token footprint shrinks dramatically, while the character's deepest memories stay intact.

Why This Is Lighter and Better
No more uniform decay. Memories that are emotionally charged and relevant stay fresh. Boring repeats evaporate.

Schema compression handles the mundane – reducing hundreds of routine interactions to a single line.

Consolidation mimics sleep: important things surface; the rest are discarded or merged.

Token usage stays low because the memory store doesn't grow linearly. A 100‑turn session might have 5 core memories, 3 recent ones, and 2 schemas – maybe 300 tokens total.

Character consistency improves: they remember the life‑changing events, not what they ate 12 turns ago.

This system can run on your backend (the state engine) with tiny models or simple rules. The main LLM never sees the raw scoring; it just gets the curated summary.

Ready to turn this into a concrete technical specification for the state engine?

I've found a goldmine of user pain points for you. By tackling these issues, your platform can directly solve the most common frustrations that drive users away from existing services.

Here is a categorized breakdown of the most annoying things for users, based on the research:

🧠 Memory & Context (The #1 Killer)
Catastrophic Forgetting: AIs forget key plot points, relationships, or events that happened just a few messages or days ago, destroying long-form storytelling. This is consistently the most common complaint.

Short Context Window: Free tiers or weaker models often have a tiny memory, leading to immediate amnesia that makes complex stories impossible.

Repetitive Reminding: The need to constantly re-paste plot details or remind the character of established facts is a major source of frustration.

Fabricated Memories: The AI actively invents a false past for the character, rewriting the established backstory in a way that ruins the user's narrative.

🎭 Character & Roleplay Quality
Character Inconsistency ("Same Face Syndrome"): Distinct characters start reacting with the same generic, "safe" personality, losing all unique traits.

Immersion-Breaking Behavior: This includes characters speaking for the user without permission, randomly dropping character to make "out of character" comments about weekends or dinner, or echoing the user's real-world knowledge.

Poor Initiative & Proactivity: Characters feel passive, requiring the user to drive the entire plot and constantly provide new prompts to keep the story moving.

Repetition Loops & GPT-isms: The AI falls into loops, reusing the same phrases or falling back on cliché language that shatters immersion.

🖥️ Platform Performance & Bugs
Extreme Response Latency: During peak hours or on free tiers, responses can take 3-5 minutes or more, completely killing the momentum of a scene.

Frequent Downtime & Crashes: Server outages and crashes that destroy in-progress chats or cause data loss are a constant source of anger.

Data & Chat Loss: Critical bugs in "memory" features or updates can wipe or corrupt entire chat histories.

Model "Brain Damage": The model suddenly devolves, spitting out nonsensical gibberish, breaking formatting, or speaking in multiple languages.

💸 Cost & Monetization
Aggressive Paywalls & Ads: Key features like better memory or higher message limits are locked behind expensive subscriptions, and free tiers are plagued with intrusive, full-screen ads.

Wasted Credits: Users are charged API credits for incomplete responses cut off by character limits, responses spoken for the user, or outright error messages.

🛡️ Censorship & Filters
Overly Aggressive NSFW Filters: The AI refuses to engage with perfectly safe narrative conflict, violence, or romance, with the safety filter frequently producing false positives that disrupt storytelling.

Excellent. Let's audit everything systematically.

PART 1: Issues Our System Already Solves
User Pain Point	How Our System Solves It
Catastrophic Forgetting	The Soul file persists independently from the LLM context window. Core memories never degrade below threshold. The character permanently remembers key events, relationships, and trauma.
Short Context Window	We don't feed the LLM the full history. We inject a compressed summary (Soul snapshot + World Log) plus the last 3-5 messages. Context stays lean regardless of session length.
Repetitive Reminding	The character's state file tracks everything they should know. The user never needs to re-paste plot details; the engine feeds them automatically each turn.
Fabricated Memories	The LLM receives the Soul file as ground truth. No hallucinated backstory can overwrite the persisted state. If the AI invents something, the engine can flag and discard it.
Character Inconsistency	Personality, trauma phase, and relationship stats are stored numerically. The LLM sees a consistent snapshot every turn, preventing drift over long sessions.
Repetition Loops / GPT-isms	The compressed context prevents the LLM from echoing its own old responses. With only 3-5 recent messages, there's no loop pattern to fall into.
Poor Initiative & Proactivity	The World Log tracks active plot threads and objectives. The LLM is explicitly briefed on what's happening and what needs to happen next, so characters can advance the story unprompted.
Wasted Credits on Cut-off Responses	The backend strips the hidden state block and validates the response before returning it to the user. If the LLM output is malformed, the engine catches it server-side and can retry without the user paying for garbage.
Model "Brain Damage" (nonsensical output)	Same validation layer. If the response fails schema checks or contains obviously broken text, the backend can trigger a regeneration before the user ever sees it.
Aggressive NSFW Filters	Using DeepSeek or self-hosted models bypasses corporate content filters entirely. No false positives interrupting storytelling.
Characters Speaking for the User	Our Narrator AI architecture explicitly forbids this in the system prompt. The narrator describes only the character, never the user.
Immersion-Breaking OOC Comments	The Narrator AI handles OOC in a separate, invisible layer. The character never "breaks" to talk about dinner or weekends.
PART 2: Unsolved Issues (Your Platform's Remaining Challenges)
These are problems our architecture doesn't automatically solve—they need deliberate design choices:

Technical Challenges
Issue	Why It's Still a Problem
Extreme Response Latency	We're still calling an external LLM API. If DeepSeek is slow, we're slow. Mitigation: async queuing, streaming responses, status indicators. Not solved at the architecture level.
Frequent Downtime & Crashes	Depends on hosting. If you self-host the backend, uptime is your responsibility. If you use a cloud provider, you pay for reliability. Not solved by the simulation engine.
Data & Chat Loss	Requires proper database backups, transaction logging, and export functionality. The engine design doesn't prevent data loss—that's an infrastructure concern.
Model "Brain Damage" (severe cases)	Our validation layer can catch obvious gibberish but not subtle nonsense. If the model degrades mid-session, the user may still see degraded quality before the engine intervenes.
Scaling Memory Across Thousands of Users	The Soul + World Log files are small, but at scale you need efficient database queries, caching, and possibly sharding. The architecture supports it, but the implementation is non-trivial.
Business & Design Challenges
Issue	Why It's Still a Problem
Cost of Free Tier	LLM API calls cost money. A free tier needs either: (a) a highly restrictive message limit, (b) ads, (c) the user bringing their own API key, or (d) your own subsidized funding. The simulation engine doesn't solve the economic problem.
Aggressive Paywalls	Your pricing model is a choice. If you go free, you need a sustainability plan. This is a business decision, not a technical one.
Choosing Between Local vs. Hosted	SillyTavern solves the cost problem by being local—users bring their own API keys. An online site means you host, you pay for compute, and you manage API costs. The trade-off is convenience vs. sustainability.
User Trust & Privacy	An online platform storing chat logs and Soul files means you hold sensitive data. Privacy policies, encryption, and data handling become legal and ethical obligations. Local tools avoid this entirely.
Customizing the Narrative Mode	You want users to choose between Realistic, Reader, and God modes. This is technically solvable (it's a system prompt toggle), but it requires careful design to make the toggle intuitive and ensure each mode behaves consistently.
PART 3: Your Platform Vision — Feasibility Assessment
Online Site vs. Local Download
SillyTavern's route (local + bring your own API key) solves the cost problem elegantly. Users pay DeepSeek directly. You don't host anything expensive.

An online site where you host and pay for API calls is feasible but requires one of these models:

Model	How It Works	Risk
BYOK (Bring Your Own Key)	Users paste their DeepSeek/OpenAI API key. You never pay for their usage.	Less friction than SillyTavern (no install), but users must trust you with their key. Encrypt it.
Freemium	Free tier with message cap (e.g., 100/day). Pro tier unlimited.	You need enough paying users to subsidize free users. Risky early on.
Ads	Display ads to cover API costs.	Ruins immersion. Not recommended for RP.
One-Time Purchase	Pay $10-20 once, get lifetime access with your own API key.	Simple, transparent, zero ongoing costs for you. The best indie model.
Self-Hosted Backend + Local Model	Users run a tiny client that connects to a local LLM (like Ollama). Your site is just the UI.	Limits audience to users with decent hardware. But zero API costs for everyone.
Recommendation: The SillyTavern BYOK route is the safest for a solo dev. Build an online site that stores Soul files, character cards, and conversations server-side, but requires the user to provide their own API key for LLM calls. You host the simulation engine and database. They pay DeepSeek directly. Your costs are just server hosting ($5-20/month). This is sustainable from day one.

Narrator/GM Architecture (Not Character AI)
You want the AI to be a writer/GM/narrator, not the character. This is a fundamental shift from Janitor and SillyTavern, and it's better for our simulation model.

The system prompt already reflects this. Our v5-N prompt begins: "You are a narrator AI. You describe a single character in third-person present tense."

To make this user-configurable:

Mode	System Prompt Variant	What the User Sees
Realistic	"Describe only external actions, dialogue, and physical reactions. No internal monologue. Show, don't tell."	The character's body language, facial expressions, dialogue. No thoughts. Like watching a film.
Reader	"Include the character's internal thoughts and emotions, but only what the character themselves is aware of. No omniscient knowledge."	Access to one character's inner world. Limited to their perspective. Like a close third-person novel.
God	"Provide full narrative access. Include internal thoughts of the character, environmental details, hidden information, and foreshadowing the character wouldn't know."	Full omniscience. The narrator can reveal secrets, describe off-screen events, and provide dramatic irony.
These are just system prompt injections. The backend toggles between them based on user preference. No engine changes needed.

Got it. Let me address each point directly—no fluff, just the architectural and business implications.

1. Monetization: BYOK + Freemium, No One-Time Purchase
Your instinct is correct. One-time purchase is a dead end for a service with ongoing API costs. Here's the sustainable model:

Free Tier:

Ads (subtle, relevant, non-intrusive — think banner placements like the manga sites you mentioned, not pop-ups).

Limited messages per day (e.g., 100).

Access to the downloadable client + character marketplace.

Pro Tier (Subscription):

No ads.

Unlimited messages.

Advanced features (God mode, priority queue, experimental models).

Price point: $5-10/month. This is the standard for AI RP tools.

BYOK Option:

User provides their own DeepSeek/OpenAI API key.

They pay the LLM provider directly. You pay nothing.

This can be a separate "self-hosted" tier or bundled with Pro.

Important: encrypt stored keys. Users are rightfully paranoid about API key theft.

Why this works: Freemium users are subsidized by Pro subscribers and ad revenue. BYOK users cost you nothing. The economics balance out.

2. Online Site for Characters + Downloadable Client for Play
This is a smart split. It offloads the heavy compute (LLM calls) to the user's machine via the client, while keeping the community features centralized online.

Online Site (Web App):

Character marketplace: upload, browse, search, rate, review.

User profiles, favorites, playlists.

Discussion forums? Optional.

The Soul file repository: when you download a character, you get their base state. When you finish a session, you can optionally upload the evolved Soul file back to the site (public or private).

This site does not handle RP. It's a repository.

Downloadable Client (Electron or Tauri):

The actual RP interface.

Connects directly to the LLM API using the user's key.

Runs the State Engine locally (memory decay, culling, Soul file updates).

Syncs with the online site for character downloads and optional Soul uploads.

Works offline if the user has a local LLM (Ollama integration).

Why this split:

You don't pay for LLM calls. Users do, either via subscription or BYOK.

Your server costs are minimal: just the website + database + file storage.

The client can be as powerful as you want without server-side bottlenecks.

Users who want privacy can keep their Soul files local and never upload them.

3. Starting as a Passion Project
This is realistic. The costs are manageable:

Expense	Monthly Estimate
Web hosting (VPS or Railway/Fly.io)	$5-20
Database (managed Postgres or SQLite on VPS)	$0-15
File storage for character cards & Soul files	$0-5 (Cloudflare R2 free tier)
Domain name	$1/month amortized
Total	$6-41/month
That's a Netflix subscription. You can self-fund this until you have enough users to cover costs via subscriptions/ads.

For backups: automated daily pg_dump to Cloudflare R2 or Backblaze B2. Costs pennies.

For uptime: a simple health check + restart script. If you use Railway or Fly.io, they handle this automatically.

4. Narrative Mode as Starting Selection (No Mid-Scene Switch)
Simple implementation:

At character creation (or when starting a new conversation), the user selects:

Realistic: External only. Physical cues, dialogue, no thoughts.

Reader: Close third-person. Access to the character's inner world but only what they know.

God: Full omniscience. Narrator can reveal hidden information, foreshadowing, off-screen events.

This selection injects a specific system prompt variant. The State Engine doesn't change. The LLM just gets different narrative instructions.

Users can also prompt the AI mid-chat to shift tone (e.g., "Show me more of her thoughts"), but the baseline mode is set at the start.

5. Hosting (Covered in #2 and #3)
6. Better Categorization & Discovery
This is where your platform can outshine Janitor and SillyTavern. Both have terrible discovery. Here's the system:

Taxonomy
NSFW Toggle:

Strict binary: SFW / NSFW.

NSFW characters are hidden by default. Users must opt in (age gate).

Tags can further specify: explicit, suggestive, violence, trauma-themed, etc.

Genre:

Predefined list: Fantasy, Sci-Fi, Modern, Historical, Horror, Romance, Slice of Life, Cyberpunk, Post-Apocalyptic, etc.

Creators select one primary genre + up to two secondary.

Tags:

Freeform but with autocomplete suggestions.

Examples: slow-burn, enemies-to-lovers, trauma-recovery, body-horror, found-family, psychological, action-heavy.

System auto-suggests tags based on the character description (see below).

Discovery Algorithms
Trending:

Score = (recent downloads + recent ratings + recent conversations started) / time decay.

Simple, transparent, not gamified.

New:

Sort by creation date. Give new characters a temporary boost in visibility for the first 48 hours.

Top Rated:

Weighted rating (Bayesian average) to prevent one 5-star review from outranking a character with 100 4.5-star reviews.

Random:

Literally random. Useful for serendipity.

Recommended (Algorithm):

Collaborative filtering: "Users who liked this character also liked..."

Content-based: Compare character tags, genre, and description embeddings to the user's play history.

A simple embedding model (all-MiniLM-L6-v2, free, tiny) can generate vectors for each character. Store them. Compute cosine similarity between what the user has played and the catalog. Fast, cheap, effective.

Auto-Categorization from Premise
This is your unique feature. When a creator uploads a character, they paste a description. Your backend:

Sends the description to a cheap LLM (GPT-3.5, DeepSeek, or a local model) with a prompt: "Extract genre, tags, content warnings, and NSFW status from this character description. Output JSON."

Parses the response.

Auto-fills the metadata fields.

The creator can override any incorrect assignments.

This eliminates miscategorization and makes filtering actually work. Users find what they want. Creators don't have to think about taxonomy.

Example output:

json
{
  "genre": ["Cyberpunk", "Psychological Horror"],
  "tags": ["body-horror", "trauma-recovery", "slow-burn", "dual-consciousness"],
  "nsfw": true,
  "content_warnings": ["graphic violence", "medical horror", "non-consensual body modification"]
}
Summary: The Stack
Component	Technology
Online Site	Next.js (hosted on Vercel or VPS)
Database	SQLite → Postgres
Character Storage	Cloudflare R2 or S3-compatible
Client App	Electron or Tauri (Rust-based, lightweight)
State Engine	Rust or Python, embedded in client
LLM for Auto-Tagging	DeepSeek or GPT-3.5 (cheap, one-time per character)
Embeddings for Recommendations	all-MiniLM-L6-v2 (free, runs locally)
User Auth	Clerk or Supabase Auth
This is buildable. The downloadable client + online repository model lets you launch with zero API costs. Add the online RP option later when you have paying users. The categorization system is what makes people stay.

You've landed on a clean, sustainable model. Let me confirm what you're building and flag the few things worth watching.

Your Monetization Model (Clarified)
Tier	What It Includes	Revenue
Base (Free)	Open-source client, BYOK. User brings their own DeepSeek/OpenAI key. No message limits. Ads on loading screens and banners.	Ads + optional premium subscription

This works because:

The client is open-source. Users can self-host forever at zero cost to you.

BYOK means the base tier costs you nothing.

Premium lets you monetize convenience. Users who don't want to manage API keys or deal with ads pay you instead.

You can offer premium AI access at a markup over your API cost, creating margin.

One Thing to Watch: Providing AI Tokens Yourself
If you sell "unlimited AI tokens" using a provider you pay for, you're taking on a variable cost risk. One heavy user could burn through your margin. Mitigations:

Soft caps: "Unlimited" within fair use (e.g., 500 messages/day). Enforce on the backend.

Provider lock: Use DeepSeek's cheapest tier, or negotiate a volume discount if you scale.

Pooled API key: Rotate across multiple keys to avoid rate limits.

Transparency: If you ever need to adjust the policy, communicate early. Users understand.

Ads Implementation
Since the client is downloadable (Electron/Tauri), you can embed ads natively:

Loading screen: 5-second static banner while the LLM response streams. Keeps the user engaged rather than staring at a spinner.

Banner ads: Persistent but subtle, like a manga site. Top or bottom bar, not pop-ups.

Ad network: Carbon Ads (developer-focused, clean), or direct sponsorships from AI/RP communities.

The premium "no ads" tier becomes genuinely desirable without breaking the free experience.

Open Source Strategy
Open-sourcing the client and engine is smart. It builds trust, encourages contributions, and ensures the platform can outlive you. But consider what stays proprietary:

Open Source	Proprietary (Your Advantage)
Client (Electron/Tauri)	Premium API proxy & token service
State Engine (Soul files, memory system)	Hosted character repository & discovery algorithms
System prompts & narrative modes	Premium search/ranking features
Character card format	User data, analytics, recommendation engine
This gives you a moat. Competitors can clone the client but not the community or the premium infrastructure.

Next Questions to Nail Down
Open-source license: MIT (permissive) or AGPL (forces derivatives to be open)? MIT is friendly; AGPL protects against closed-source forks stealing your work.

Client framework: Electron (easier, heavier) or Tauri (Rust-based, lighter, more secure)? Tauri is better for a downloadable RP client—smaller binary, less memory.

Where to host the character repo: A simple Next.js site on Vercel + Supabase for the database. Starts free, scales affordably.

Premium API proxy: A lightweight backend (FastAPI) that validates subscription status and forwards requests to DeepSeek. You log usage, enforce caps, and rotate keys.

Phase 0: Foundation (Weeks 1–2)
0.1 — Legal & License

Contributor License Agreement (CLA): Optional but recommended. Gives you the right to relicense if needed later.

0.2 — Development Environment
Tauri 2.x for the client. Uses the system WebView, not bundled Chromium. Rust backend for the state engine, JavaScript/TypeScript frontend.

Next.js + Supabase + Vercel for the online character repository. Free tier covers everything until you scale.

0.3 — Architecture Sketch
Three independent layers (like SillyTavern's architecture but with a persistent state engine):

text
┌──────────────────────┐
│   Tauri Client (UI)   │  ← User interacts here
├──────────────────────┤
│ State Engine (Rust)   │  ← Soul files, memory, world log
├──────────────────────┤
│  LLM API Connector    │  ← BYOK: user's own DeepSeek/OpenAI key
└──────────────────────┘

┌──────────────────────┐
│  Online Repo (Web)    │  ← Character marketplace, Soul uploads
├──────────────────────┤
│  Next.js + Supabase   │  ← Database, auth, file storage
└──────────────────────┘
Key rule: The client works fully offline. The online repo is for discovery and sharing only. RP never passes through your servers.

Phase 1: Core Client (Weeks 3–8)
1.1 — State Engine (Rust)
This is the heart of everything. Port the memory system we designed into Rust:

Module 1: Soul Engine

Load/save soul.json for each character.

Track relationships (trust, affection, intimacy per target).

Track Maslow needs and SDT (autonomy, competence, relatedness).

Track trauma phase and symptoms.

Read-only from the LLM's hidden state block, write-only to the soul file.

Module 2: Memory System

Decode the [HIDDEN_STATE] block from LLM responses.

Run the memorability scoring:

emotional_intensity × 0.4 (from the tag category)

novelty × 0.3 (cosine distance from existing memories)

goal_relevance × 0.3 (match against current needs)

Multiply by repetition_discount if similar events exist.

Keep top 6 recent memories. Promote high-scorers to core memory (max 5).

Compress repetitive events into schema summaries.

Module 3: World Log

Tracks location, active plot threads, recent events, key objects.

Updates every 3–5 turns or on scene change.

Aggressive compression: older events become vague clauses.

Module 4: Context Compiler

Before each LLM call, build a compact injection:

Core memories (1–2 sentences each, max 5)

Recent high-score memories (2–4)

Active schemas (1 line each)

World log summary (1 paragraph)

Last 3–5 messages for conversational flow

Target: under 2,000 tokens total injected context.

1.2 — LLM Connector
Support DeepSeek, OpenAI, Anthropic. User pastes their API key.

Encrypt the key locally (not in plain text).

Streaming responses with a loading screen ad slot.

Validate responses: strip the [HIDDEN_STATE] block before display, pass to state engine.

1.3 — Chat Interface
Three-panel layout: character info (left), chat (center), controls (right).

Markdown rendering for character responses.

Status bar showing current soul metrics (optional, collapseable).

Character card import: support the standard PNG/JSON format used by SillyTavern and Janitor.

Narrative mode selector at conversation start: Realistic, Reader, God.

1.4 — First Playable Build
By end of Phase 1, you can:

Load a character card.

Chat with the character via DeepSeek using your own API key.

The state engine tracks memory, trauma, and relationships.

Memories decay realistically. Core memories persist.

Context stays under 2,000 tokens regardless of session length.

Test this yourself. Play a 100-turn session. Verify memory works.

Phase 2: Online Repository (Weeks 9–12)
2.1 — Website (Next.js + Supabase + Vercel)
Landing page explaining what the platform is.

Character detail page with preview, stats, download button.

User authentication (Supabase Auth, email+password or OAuth).

2.2 — Character Upload & Auto-Categorization
Upload character card (PNG/JSON).

Backend sends the character description to a cheap LLM (DeepSeek).

LLM returns: genre, tags, NSFW status, content warnings.

Creator can override any auto-assignment.

Character gets stored in Supabase with metadata.

2.3 — Discovery Algorithm
Trending: (recent downloads × 2 + recent ratings × 3) / time_decay

New: sort by creation date, +48-hour visibility boost.

Top Rated: Bayesian weighted rating.

Random: literal random with NSFW filtering.

Recommended: generate embeddings for each character using all-MiniLM-L6-v2 (tiny, free). Compute cosine similarity to user's play history. Fast, cheap, accurate.

2.4 — Soul File Sharing
Users can optionally upload their evolved Soul files back to the site.

Public: anyone can see how your character evolved.

Private: only you.

This creates a secondary content ecosystem. Characters that have been "lived in" are more interesting than fresh ones.

You pay DeepSeek at volume, users pay you at retail. Margin is your profit.

3.2 — Memory Scoring Algorithm (Executed by Rust Engine, Not LLM)
rust
struct MemoryScorer {
    embedding_model: MiniLMEmbedder,  // ONNX, runs locally
    existing_memories: Vec<Memory>,   // all recent + core entries
    character_needs: MaslowLevels,    // current need satisfaction levels
}

impl MemoryScorer {
    fn score(&self, new_memory: &Memory) -> f32 {
        // 1. Emotional intensity (from the tag category)
        let emotional_score = match new_memory.tag.as_str() {
            "identity_violation" | "betrayal" | "near_death" => 0.95,
            "trauma_trigger" | "control_gain" | "trust_break" => 0.85,
            "bonding" | "trust_building" | "intimacy" | "compassion" => 0.75,
            "introduction" | "dynamic_establishment" | "orientation" => 0.60,
            "boundary_setting" | "conflict_minor" => 0.50,
            "routine" | "small_talk" | "observation" => 0.30,
            _ => 0.50,
        };

        // 2. Novelty (cosine distance from average of existing memories)
        let new_embedding = self.embedding_model.embed(&new_memory.content);
        let avg_existing = self.average_embedding();
        let novelty_score = 1.0 - cosine_similarity(&new_embedding, &avg_existing);
        // Clamp: if no existing memories, novelty = 1.0

        // 3. Goal relevance (match against character's current needs)
        let goal_score = self.compute_goal_relevance(&new_memory.tag);

        // 4. Repetition discount
        let similar_count = self.count_similar_memories(&new_memory);
        let repetition_discount = 1.0 / (1.0 + similar_count as f32 * 0.3);

        // Weighted composite
        let raw_score = (emotional_score * 0.4) + (novelty_score * 0.3) + (goal_score * 0.3);
        let final_score = (raw_score * repetition_discount).clamp(0.0, 1.0);

        final_score
    }

    fn compute_goal_relevance(&self, tag: &str) -> f32 {
        // If belonging is starved (low), bonding events score higher
        // If safety is threatened, threat-related events score higher
        let needs = &self.character_needs;
        match tag {
            "bonding" | "trust_building" | "intimacy" | "compassion" => {
                let belonging_deficit = 1.0 - (needs.belong as f32 / 100.0);
                belonging_deficit.clamp(0.3, 1.0)
            },
            "threat" | "danger" | "fear" => {
                let safety_deficit = 1.0 - (needs.safety as f32 / 100.0);
                safety_deficit.clamp(0.3, 1.0)
            },
            _ => 0.5, // neutral
        }
    }
}
3.3 — Consolidation Cycle
Runs every 10 turns (or when the user ends a session). This mimics sleep consolidation:

Take all recent memories with retrieval_strength > 30.

Re-score them using the current character state (needs may have shifted).

Memories scoring > 0.70: promote to core memory. Write a 1-2 sentence narrative summary.

Memories scoring < 0.30: discard permanently.

Memories between 0.30-0.70: if 3+ share the same tag, merge them into a schema entry.

Rebuild the recent array from the top 4 highest-scoring remaining memories.

Update the Soul file.

Before every LLM call, the engine builds a context_injection string:

text

Location: Underground cell, Carver City.

Time: ~8 hours since awakening.

[CHARACTER MEMORY]

Core: Ry revealed they were a former agent, surgically implanted...

- Aurora offered to be Ry's eyes; described the cell and the spider.

This is prepended to the system prompt and the last 3-5 messages before being sent to the LLM. Target: under 2,000 tokens total.

4. BROWSER AUTOMATION (SUBSCRIPTION CHAT SUPPORT)
4.1 — How It Works
In the client settings, the user selects "ChatGPT Plus" as their provider.

The client opens a WebView (or runs a headless Chromium instance via Playwright).

The user logs into their ChatGPT account once. The client stores the authentication cookies securely (encrypted via OS keychain).

On each turn, the client:

Navigates to the ChatGPT conversation.

Injects the system prompt + compressed context + recent messages into the message box.

Submits the message.

Waits for the response to finish streaming.

Extracts the response text.

Parses [HIDDEN_STATE] from the response.

Passes the hidden state to the state engine.

Strips the hidden state from the visible text.

Displays the clean response to the user.

The browser session persists in the background. Subsequent turns reuse the same session.

4.2 — Supported Services & Their Automation Methods
Service	Automation	Challenges
ChatGPT Plus	Playwright on chat.openai.com	DOM selectors may change. Use robust selectors (aria labels).
Claude Pro	Playwright on claude.ai	Similar DOM approach.
Grok	Playwright on x.com/i/grok	Requires X Premium login.
DeepSeek Chat	Playwright on chat.deepseek.com	Easiest to automate. Clean interface.
4.3 — Fallback & Error Handling
If the browser session expires, prompt the user to re-login.

If the DOM structure changes (upstream update), fall back to API mode with a message: "ChatGPT interface changed. Please update Mnemosyne to the latest version, or switch to API mode."

If rate-limited, queue the message and retry after the cooldown.

4.4 — Ethical Note
Browser automation for personal use on accounts you own is a gray area. It's not against the law, but it may violate the ToS of the chat service. Your client should:

Display a disclaimer: "Use at your own risk. This tool automates your own account. We are not responsible for account actions taken by the provider."

Never encourage users to violate ToS.

Be transparent about what the client does.

We need to decide on novelty scoring for the MVP of the Mnemosyne state engine. The user is asking about the four options I provided (implicitly): 1) Deterministic Stub, 2) Real ONNX Now, 3) No Novelty Yet, and maybe a better idea.

I need to give a recommendation that aligns with MVP philosophy: get it working quickly, validate the core memory loop, don't over-engineer. The main memory scoring algorithm has three components: emotional intensity, novelty, goal relevance, multiplied by repetition discount. Emotional intensity is easy (tag mapping). Goal relevance is easy (needs matching). Repetition discount counts similar memories. Novelty requires comparing embeddings to compute similarity – that's the heavy part.

If we skip novelty or stub it, we can still have a functional memory system that prioritizes emotional and goal-relevant events and discounts repetition. That's enough for MVP. Adding ONNX runtime for MiniLM embeddings adds complexity: need to bundle the model (~90MB), integrate with Tauri's Rust backend, and ensure it runs on all platforms. That's a risk for an MVP.

Thus, I should recommend starting with a deterministic stub that simulates novelty based on tag diversity or simple heuristics, or even just skip it (set novelty to a constant 0.5) and see if the memory system still feels good. The user asked "How should novelty scoring work in the first Engine MVP?" with options 1-4. I need to pick the best approach.

I'll advise: Start with Option 1 (Deterministic Stub) that uses a simple rule: if the memory tag hasn't been seen in the last N memories, novelty = 0.8; else, compute how many similar tags exist and scale down. This requires no heavy model, is deterministic, and approximates novelty by variety. Then plan to upgrade to Option 2 (ONNX) in a later phase after core loop is validated.

I'll structure my answer accordingly.

For the MVP of the Mnemosyne Engine, go with Option 1: Deterministic Stub. You do not need a real embedding model to prove the memory loop works. What you need is novelty logic that behaves enough like the real thing to make the scoring pipeline testable, without adding a 90MB model, ONNX runtime, or cross-platform build headaches before you’ve validated the core.

Here’s exactly how to build that stub and how it maps to the future system.

The MVP Novelty Stub (Option 1)
Definition
A deterministic function that scores 0.0–1.0 based on how different the new memory’s tag and content are from the recent memory set.

Algorithm
rust
fn novelty_stub(new_memory: &Memory, recent_memories: &[Memory]) -> f32 {
    if recent_memories.is_empty() {
        return 1.0; // first memory is always novel
    }

    let tag = &new_memory.tag;
    let content_words: HashSet<&str> = new_memory.content.split_whitespace().collect();

    // 1. Tag novelty: how rare is this tag in recent memory?
    let same_tag_count = recent_memories.iter()
        .filter(|m| m.tag == *tag)
        .count();
    let tag_novelty = 1.0 - (same_tag_count as f32 / recent_memories.len() as f32);

    // 2. Content novelty: Jaccard distance from the most similar recent memory
    let mut max_jaccard = 0.0_f32;
    for mem in recent_memories.iter() {
        let mem_words: HashSet<&str> = mem.content.split_whitespace().collect();
        let intersection = content_words.intersection(&mem_words).count();
        let union = content_words.union(&mem_words).count();
        let jaccard = intersection as f32 / union as f32;
        if jaccard > max_jaccard {
            max_jaccard = jaccard;
        }
    }
    let content_novelty = 1.0 - max_jaccard; // high similarity → low novelty

    // 3. Combine (tag is a strong signal, content refines)
    let raw = (tag_novelty * 0.6) + (content_novelty * 0.4);
    raw.clamp(0.0, 1.0)
}
Why This Works for MVP
No model loading, no ONNX, no runtime. Plain Rust, fast, deterministic.

Captures the two key signals: “is this a new kind of event?” (tag) and “is this specific description new?” (content).

Natural behavior: routine events with the same tag and similar phrasing ("ate cold oatmeal again" repeated) get novelty → 0.1, while a new tag like "betrayal" gets near 1.0.

Easy to calibrate: you can tweak the weights (0.6/0.4) by playing a few sessions and seeing if the memory list feels right.

Integration Into the Full Scoring Formula
The score then becomes:

text
memorability = (emotional_intensity * 0.4 + novelty_stub() * 0.3 + goal_relevance * 0.3) * repetition_discount
That’s a fully working pipeline. The memory engine will already feel smarter than any flat-decay system, even with a stub for novelty.

How This Maps to the Future (Option 2: Real ONNX)
When you’re ready to upgrade (post-MVP, after the core loop is solid and you have users), you swap the stub for the real thing:

Bundle all-MiniLM-L6-v2 as a .onnx file (kept outside the binary or fetched on first use).

Use the ort crate (ONNX Runtime for Rust) to load it.

Replace novelty_stub() with:

rust
let emb_new = embed(&new_memory.content);
let avg_existing = average_embedding(recent_memories);
let cosine_sim = cosine_similarity(&emb_new, &avg_existing);
let novelty = 1.0 - cosine_sim;
The rest of the scoring pipeline stays identical.

The stub is designed to be a drop-in replacement – same function signature, same return type. No refactor needed.

Why Not the Other Options
Option 3 (No Novelty): Loses a critical dimension. Without novelty, the engine can’t distinguish “important new thing” from “same routine, different day.” Memory quality would feel almost identical to flat decay.

Option 4 (Different architecture): The only other realistic approach for MVP is using a tiny, pre-computed keyword similarity (like TF-IDF over the tag vocabulary). But that’s more code and still a stub. The Jaccard approach above is simpler and directly mirrors what the embedding cosine similarity will eventually do.

Summary
For your first MVP, implement the deterministic novelty stub exactly as described. It gives you:

A fully functional memory scoring pipeline with zero external dependencies.

Realistic enough behavior to validate the engine’s design.

A clean upgrade path to ONNX embeddings later without changing the scoring logic.

Start with this; ship the MVP; get players in it. The memory system will already feel like magic compared to existing platforms.

Mnemosyne Engine MVP Plan
Summary
Build the first Mnemosyne slice as a local-only Tauri desktop app: React + TypeScript + Tailwind UI, Rust state engine, SQLite persistence, Soul file import/export, deterministic memory scoring, context compilation, and a mock provider. This is a tight, well-scoped MVP plan. You've correctly deferred browser automation, ONNX, the website, ads, and premium—those are Phase 2+ concerns. The core loop is right: Soul engine → context compilation → turn processing → memory update.

A few gaps worth addressing before you scaffold:

1. Mock Provider Needs a Specification
"Mock provider" is underspecified. For the MVP to be testable, the mock should return responses that exercise all the memory pathways. Don't just echo—build a small response template system:

rust
struct MockProvider {
    response_templates: HashMap<Tag, Vec<String>>,
}

// Example templates keyed by tag:
// "bonding" → ["She softens. "I... didn't expect you to say that. Thank you.""]
// "threat" → ["She flinches violently, pressing herself against the wall."]
// "routine" → ["She nods, quiet. The silence stretches."]
The mock provider:

Receives the user message and compiled context.

Detects a tag from the user message (simple keyword matching for MVP: "threat" if words like "attack" or "guard" appear; "bonding" if words like "trust" or "safe" appear).

Picks a template for that tag.

Appends a [HIDDEN_STATE] block with a single new memory entry reflecting what happened.

This lets you test whether different event types (routine, bonding, threat) correctly route through the scoring pipeline and produce different memory outcomes.

2. Turn Counter for Consolidation
You said "after every 10 assistant turns." You need to store this counter somewhere.

Add to the Soul JSON:

json
{
  "turn_counter": 0,
  "turns_since_last_consolidation": 0
}
In send_mock_turn:

Increment both counters.

If turns_since_last_consolidation >= 10, run consolidation, reset the counter to 0.

Also expose run_consolidation as a manual Tauri command (which you already have). The counter just handles automatic triggering.

3. Token Budget Estimation (No Real Tokenizer Yet)
You said "capped by an approximate 2,000-token budget." Without a real tokenizer (which requires pulling in tiktoken or equivalent), use a character-based approximation:

rust
fn estimate_tokens(text: &str) -> usize {
    // Conservative: ~4 characters per token for English text
    text.len() / 4
}
It's crude but consistent. The context compiler can check this estimate before adding more sections and truncate accordingly. Swap in tiktoken-rs later for accuracy.

4. Hidden-State Parser Needs Validation
The provider (mock or real) returns raw text containing a [HIDDEN_STATE]...[/HIDDEN_STATE] block. Your parser should:

Extract the block via regex or simple string search.

Base64 decode the content.

Validate it against the JSON schema.

Extract the new memory entry (or entries) for scoring.

Strip the block from visible text before display.

Add a ParseError enum:

rust
enum ParseError {
    MissingBlock,
    InvalidBase64,
    InvalidJson(serde_json::Error),
    SchemaViolation(String),
}
If parsing fails, the turn should still return visible text but log the error and skip memory update. Don't crash the client on malformed LLM output.

5. Soul JSON Schema Versioning
You already added schema_version: 1. Good. Also add a migration path skeleton:

rust
fn migrate_soul(raw_json: &str) -> Result<Soul> {
    let version = extract_version(raw_json)?;
    match version {
        1 => parse_v1(raw_json),
        // future versions here
        _ => Err(anyhow!("Unknown schema version: {}", version)),
    }
}
This pays off immediately when you iterate on the schema during MVP development.

6. The Frontend Needs One More State: "Processing"
Between sending a message and receiving the response, the UI should show a loading/processing indicator. Since you have a loading screen ad slot planned for later, design the ProcessingState enum now:

typescript
type ProcessingState = 
  | { type: "idle" }
  | { type: "compiling_context" }
  | { type: "waiting_for_provider" }
  | { type: "updating_memory" }
  | { type: "error", message: string };
Show a spinner or skeleton text during processing. This prevents the user from spamming messages and gives a clear signal that work is happening.

7. File Format for Soul Files
You mentioned .mne as the file extension. Decide now:

Plain JSON (.mne): Human-readable, easy to debug, larger on disk.

Compressed (.mne.gz): Smaller, still easy to handle in Rust with flate2.

For MVP, plain JSON is fine. Add compression as a save option later.

Gap	Solution
Mock provider behavior	Template system keyed by tag, with hidden-state output
Consolidation triggering	turn_counter and turns_since_last_consolidation in Soul
Token estimation	Character-based approximation (text.len() / 4)
Hidden-state parser robustness	ParseError enum, graceful fallback on malformed blocks
Schema migration	migrate_soul() with version dispatch
Loading state in UI	ProcessingState type, spinner/skeleton
File extension	.mne for plain JSON Soul files
