The "Soul" struct contains the character's psychological profile, memory, and world log. The "SettingSoul" is a separate entity for the shared world state. The user's question is about where the sexual response system should reside. It's a state that affects the character's perception and reaction, and it's mutable. It should probably be part of the Soul, as it relates to the character's internal state and can influence the narrator's description. However, it might also be considered a temporary "stat" that could be separate. I need to clarify that the Soul is the correct place for such a system, as it's about the character's mutable psychological and physiological state. I'll also need to consider how gear/equipment might be tracked separately, as part of the world log or a separate inventory system.

That's an excellent architectural question. In Mnemosyne's design, the sexual response model belongs inside the Soul. Here's exactly why, and how it connects to everything else.

What Lives in the Soul vs. Everything Else
Based on the architecture you've built (soul.rs, setting.rs), here's the clear separation:

Lives In...	What It Contains	Why
Soul	Character identity, psyche, memory, relationships, trauma, needs	Internal to the character. Defines who they are and how they react. Mutable over time.
SettingSoul	World log, location, active plots, time elapsed	Shared world state. Doesn't change based on which character is present.
Future "Stats" (Gear/Equipment)	Inventory, worn items, physical status flags	External to identity. Can be swapped in/out of a setting without changing the character.
The sexual response system is fundamentally a physiological and psychological reaction pattern. It's internal. It defines how the character responds to stimuli. That's the Soul's job. So the ArousalState struct belongs alongside GlobalState and TraumaState as a new field in the Soul.

Where Exactly in the Soul Schema
In soul.rs, the Soul struct currently has:

rust
pub struct Soul {
    pub global: GlobalState,      // personality, needs
    pub trauma: TraumaState,      // trauma phase, symptoms
    pub memory: MemoryStore,     // core, recent, schemas
    pub relationships: HashMap<String, Relationship>,  // per-character bonds
    pub world: WorldLog,          // the character's perception of the world
    // ...
}
The sexual response system should go as:

rust
pub struct Soul {
    pub global: GlobalState,
    pub trauma: TraumaState,
    pub arousal: ArousalState,     // ← NEW
    pub memory: MemoryStore,
    pub relationships: HashMap<String, Relationship>,
    pub world: WorldLog,
    // ...
}
ArousalState itself would look something like:

rust
#[derive(Debug, Clone, Serialize, Deserialize, PartialEq)]
pub struct ArousalState {
    pub level: f32,                    // 0-100 (neutral to orgasm)
    pub frustration: f32,             // 0-100 (edging denial pressure)
    pub refractory_until: Option<i64>, // timestamp when refractory period ends (male)
    pub denial_active: bool,           // is orgasm being denied by external control?
    pub denial_duration_turns: u64,    // how long has denial been active?
    pub sensitivity_modifier: f32,     // multiplier from prolonged denial/edging
    pub discomfort: f32,              // 0-100 (post-orgasm overstimulation pain)
    pub last_orgasm_timestamp: Option<i64>,
    pub orgasm_count_this_session: u32,
}
This is internal to the character. When you export a Soul (.mne file), the arousal state exports with it. When you import a character into a new Setting, their arousal baseline comes along—just like their trauma phase or trust levels.

What Stays Separate: Gear, Equipment, Physical Stats
You also asked about gear and equipment. Those are not part of the Soul. They're external. Here's the reasoning:

System	Example	Storage
Soul (Internal)	Trust, fear, arousal, trauma phase, core memories, personality	soul.mne or soul.json
Setting (Shared)	Location, plot, time, NPCs present	setting.json + world_log.json
Stats/Equipment (External)	Weapons, armor, health potions, a restraining device, a vibrator used in a scene	Future: character_state.json within a setting, or a gear field on the SettingSoul
Gear is situation-specific. A character might have a sword in one setting and a flashlight in another. Their Soul doesn't change because they picked up a different object. So gear belongs in a per-setting character state file, not in the Soul.

You might eventually add a GearState or Inventory struct that sits between the Setting and the Soul:

text
Setting (world_log, active_characters_list)
├── CharacterSlot: Aurora
│   ├── Soul (psyche, memory, arousal, relationships)
│   └── Gear (current inventory, equipped items, physical status flags)
├── CharacterSlot: Ry
│   ├── Soul
│   └── Gear
But for the MVP, gear tracking can be handled narratively (the narrator simply describes what the character is carrying, and the world log tracks key objects). The key_objects field in WorldLog already does a light version of this.

How the Engine Uses ArousalState
The context compiler (context_compiler.rs) already injects relationship stats and memory into the LLM prompt. It would also inject the current arousal state as a short summary:

text
[AROUSAL STATE]
Level: 45/100 (READY). Edging: inactive. Sensitivity: normal.
The LLM sees this and narrates accordingly—describing physical signs appropriate to the READY phase, not jumping to orgasm. The hidden state parser (hidden_state.rs) can accept optional arousal deltas in the hidden state JSON:

json
{
  "memory": "He kissed her neck softly",
  "tag": "intimacy",
  "arousal_delta": 8.0
}
The engine applies the arousal_delta, checks the stimulus intensity table, applies the denial_bonus multiplier if active, and caps at PEAK during denial mode. The arousal level gate-keeps what the narrator is allowed to describe. The LLM can't override it.

Summary
Arousal state is part of the Soul. It's internal, psychological/physiological, and travels with the character.

Gear and equipment are separate. They're external, setting-specific, and belong in a per-setting character state file or inventory system (future feature).

The engine enforces the arousal ladder. The LLM doesn't guess arousal changes; the engine processes stimulus scores deterministically and tells the narrator what state the character is in.

This separation keeps the Soul focused on identity and internal state, while leaving room for physical/external systems to be added without refactoring the core engine.

Here's a self-contained prompt you can send to your coding agent. It includes the arousal model, the struct definitions, the logic flow, system prompt updates, and test requirements—everything needed to implement the feature.

markdown
## Mnemosyne: Sexual Response Model Implementation

### Overview
Add a realistic, science-based arousal state machine to the Soul engine. This prevents the AI narrator from jumping from "neutral" to "orgasm" in a single turn; it forces a physiologically plausible buildup and decay, supports edging/denial, forced orgasms, and refractory periods.

### 1. New Struct: `ArousalState`
Add to `state_engine/src/soul.rs`:

```rust
#[derive(Debug, Clone, Serialize, Deserialize, PartialEq)]
pub struct ArousalState {
    pub level: f32,                      // 0-100 (NEUTRAL to ORGASM/RELEASE)
    pub frustration: f32,               // 0-100 (builds during edging/denial)
    pub refractory_until: Option<i64>,  // unix timestamp until refractory ends (male)
    pub denial_active: bool,            // is orgasm being externally suppressed?
    pub denial_duration_turns: u64,     // consecutive turns denial has been active
    pub sensitivity_modifier: f32,      // multiplier from prolonged denial (>1.0 increases arousal gain)
    pub discomfort: f32,               // 0-100 (post-orgasm overstimulation pain)
    pub last_orgasm_timestamp: Option<i64>,
    pub orgasm_count_this_session: u32,
}
Also add a sex field to the Soul’s identity/global section: pub sex: Sex where Sex is an enum Male, Female, Other. This dictates refractory rules.

2. Integrate Into Soul
Add pub arousal: ArousalState to the Soul struct.

In new_default_soul(...), initialize arousal with level: 0.0, frustration: 0.0, etc.

Update all Soul construction sites (tests, mocks) to include the new field.

3. Arousal Logic Module
Create a new module state_engine/src/arousal.rs exposing:

rust
pub fn apply_arousal_delta(soul: &mut Soul, delta: f32, current_time: i64) {
    // 1. If refractory_until is Some and current_time < refractory_until:
    //    Stimulus cannot raise arousal; instead increase discomfort by |delta|*0.5.
    //    Return early.
    // 2. Apply sensitivity_modifier: effective_delta = delta * soul.arousal.sensitivity_modifier.
    // 3. Repetition damping: if the same tag/stimulus type appears repeatedly in recent turns,
    //    apply a diminishing-returns factor (0.5) – implementation suggestion: check the tag
    //    of the last hidden state event; if it's identical, dampen.
    // 4. Add effective_delta to soul.arousal.level, clamp to 0.0-100.0.
    // 5. If denial_active && level > 75.0 (PEAK threshold):
    //    Set level = 75.0, add excess to frustration.
    //    Increase denial_duration_turns.
    // 6. If level >= 100.0: trigger orgasm.
    //    - Set level = 0.0 for male; for female, reset to 55.0-70.0 (depending on arousal model).
    //    - Increment orgasm_count_this_session.
    //    - Set last_orgasm_timestamp.
    //    - For male, set refractory_until = current_time + refractory_duration (e.g., 10-30 min in seconds).
    //    - Reset denial_duration_turns, frustration (partial).
    // 7. If delta == 0.0 (no stimulus): apply passive decay of -3 to -8 points.
    //    Decay rate depends on current level: higher decay when highly aroused.
}
Also add a helper to reset denial:

rust
pub fn reset_denial(soul: &mut Soul) {
    soul.arousal.denial_active = false;
    soul.arousal.denial_duration_turns = 0;
    soul.arousal.sensitivity_modifier = 1.0;
}
And a helper to update sensitivity modifier based on denial duration (call after each turn where denial is active):

rust
pub fn update_denial_bonus(soul: &mut Soul) {
    if soul.arousal.denial_active {
        soul.arousal.sensitivity_modifier = 1.0 + (soul.arousal.denial_duration_turns as f32 * 0.02).min(1.0);
    }
}
4. Hidden State Parser Updates
In hidden_state.rs, extend the HiddenState struct with optional fields:

rust
pub struct HiddenState {
    // existing fields...
    pub arousal_delta: Option<f32>,
    pub arousal_tag: Option<String>,   // to detect repetition
    pub denial_toggle: Option<bool>,   // true = activate denial, false = deactivate
}
After applying trust/affection deltas, call arousal::apply_arousal_delta with the parsed delta and current timestamp. Also handle denial_toggle.

5. Context Compiler Update
In context_compiler.rs, add a line to the compiled context:

text
[AROUSAL]
Level: 45/100 (READY) | Frustration: 12 | Denial: inactive
Select the phase label dynamically: NEUTRAL (0-10), AWARE (11-25), WARM (26-40), READY (41-60), PLATEAU (61-75), PEAK (76-90), ORGASM (91-100).

6. System Prompt Update
Append to the base narrator prompt (NARRATOR_SYSTEM_PROMPT in tauri.ts or Rust constants) the stimulus intensity guidelines:

text
## AROUSAL GUIDELINES
The character has a hidden arousal level (0-100). Your task is to narrate physical changes appropriate to the phase and assign a delta (0-60) based on stimulus intensity:
- Light clothed touch (shoulder, hand): +2 to +5
- Deep kiss, gentle breast touch: +8 to +15
- Direct genital stimulation (moderate): +15 to +25
- Intense, prolonged genital stimulation: +30 to +40
- Orgasm-specific trigger: +40 to +60
- Repetitive same action: reduce delta by 50%
You must NEVER skip phases. Describe at least two intermediate physical changes before orgasm. Include the delta in the hidden state as "arousal_delta": <number>.
7. Tests
Add unit tests in state_engine/src/arousal.rs:

test_basic_buildup: apply small deltas, verify level increases correctly.

test_denial_cap: set denial_active, apply large delta, verify level capped at 75 and frustration increased.

test_male_refractory: trigger orgasm for male, then apply a stimulus before refractory_until, verify level stays 0 and discomfort rises.

test_female_multiorgasmic: trigger orgasm for female, verify level resets to ~60, not 0.

test_edging_bonus: increase denial_duration_turns, verify sensitivity_modifier grows.

test_decay: apply a delta, then apply zero delta, verify level decreases by 3-8.

Integration test in tests/integration.rs:

Simulate a gentle scene (10 turns) with escalating touch, confirm level progresses from NEUTRAL to PLATEAU, no sudden orgasm.

I've gathered everything from our entire conversation, the repo's last known state, and the architecture decisions. Here are both documents.

Mnemosyne Program Design Document ("The Bible")
1. Project Identity
Name: Mnemosyne
Pronunciation: "neh-MOSS-uh-nee"
Meaning: Greek goddess of memory, mother of the Muses.
Tagline: Remember who you are.
License: AGPL-3.0-or-later
Repository: https://github.com/RhyGPU/mnemosyne

2. Vision & Unique Selling Points
Mnemosyne is a narrator/GM engine for AI-assisted roleplay, not a character impersonator. It solves the fundamental problem of AI amnesia and emotional shallowness through a persistent, scientifically-modeled "Soul" file that evolves over time.

Unlike Janitor AI, SillyTavern, or ChatGPT-based RP, Mnemosyne:

Separates the narrator AI from the character's mind.

Maintains a persistent memory system with realistic forgetting, consolidation, and schema formation.

Tracks psychological stats (trust, trauma, needs) with speed‑gated progression.

Supports multi‑character and multi‑setting play.

Is open source (AGPL) and runs locally, with the user bringing their own API keys or subscription chat accounts.

3. Core Architecture
3.1 High‑Level Data Flow
text
User Message
    |
    v
State Engine (Rust) compiles context
    |  (Soul summary, World Log, recent messages)
    v
System Prompt (Narrator + Mode) + Context + User Message
    |
    v
LLM API (DeepSeek, OpenAI, etc.)
    |
    v
LLM Response: Third‑person narration + [HIDDEN STATE] block
    |
    v
Parser (Rust) extracts hidden state
    |
    v
Engine applies deltas (trust, affection, arousal)
    |
    v
Memory scorer assigns salience & novelty
    |
    v
If turn count % 10 == 0: run consolidation
    |
    v
Soul file updated, conversation stored in SQLite
    |
    v
Clean narration (no hidden block) shown to user
3.2 Dual‑System Memory (Character + Story)
System 1 (Soul): Private character memory. Tracks relationships, trauma, arousal, core memories, recent memories, schemas. Evolves with every interaction.

System 2 (World Log + Context Compiler): Story tracker. Location, active plots, key objects, shared recent events. Compressed over time.

The LLM never sees raw state JSON. Instead, the context compiler injects a curated summary.

3.3 Narrative Modes
The user selects one of four modes at session start, injected as a modifier to the base narrator prompt:

Realistic – External only (film camera)

Reader – Close third‑person, limited to character's knowledge

God – Full omniscience

Custom – User provides their own system prompt

4. The Soul File (soul.mne or .soul.json)
4.1 Identity (Static)
character_name, appearance, setting, personality, backstory

These do not auto‑update; they are the starting foundation.

4.2 Psychological State (Mutable)
Category	Variables	Speed
Global	fear_baseline, resolve, shame, openness, dev_stage, attach_style	Slow
Needs (Maslow)	phys, safety, belong, esteem, actual	Medium
SDT	autonomy, competence, relatedness	Medium
Trauma	phase, hypervigilance, flashbacks, numbing, avoidance	Slow
Relationships	trust, affection, intimacy, passion, commitment, fear, desire per target	Fast
4.3 Memory Store
Core (long‑term): Up to 5 narrative summaries of high‑salience events (score >0.70). Never deleted unless displaced by higher‑score memories.

Recent (short‑term): Up to 4 detailed entries with retrieval strengths that decay.

Schemas: Merged routines (e.g., "prison_routine: days in cell, cold meals") to compress repetitive events.

4.4 Memory Scoring
Deterministic Rust code (not the LLM) calculates a memorability score:

text
score = (emotional_intensity × 0.4) + (novelty × 0.3) + (goal_relevance × 0.3)
        × repetition_discount
emotional_intensity: from tag mapping (e.g., betrayal = 0.95, routine = 0.30)

novelty: Jaccard distance from existing memories (MVP) → embeddings (future)

goal_relevance: matches character's current need deficits

repetition_discount: diminishes if same tag/content already frequently recorded

4.5 Consolidation (Every 10 Turns)
Re‑score recent memories with updated needs.

Promote >0.70 → core (write narrative summary).

Discard <0.30.

Merge 3+ same‑tag middle‑range memories into a schema.

Keep top 4 recent memories.

4.6 Arousal State Machine (Sexual Response Model)
Tracks arousal_level (0–100) across phases: NEUTRAL → AWARE → WARM → READY → PLATEAU → PEAK → ORGASM.

Stimulus intensity mapped to delta (+2 to +60) with repetition damping.

Supports edging (cap at PEAK with frustration buildup, sensitivity modifier), denial, forced orgasms, and male/female refractory differences.

Stored in the Soul, injected into context as a one‑line summary.

5. The Setting Soul (Multi‑Character)
Setting Souls are containers that hold:

A shared World Log (location, plot threads, time, key objects)

A shared conversation history (SQLite)

Multiple character Souls (each independent)

Active character detection: scan last 5–10 messages for character names; the context compiler then injects memories and relationships for all present characters.

6. Provider Architecture
API Providers: Direct HTTP calls (reqwest) to DeepSeek, OpenAI, OpenRouter, etc. Streaming via Tauri events.

Browser Automation (Phase 5): Playwright‑based connector for subscription chat services (ChatGPT Plus, Claude Pro) to piggyback on existing subscriptions.

Mock Provider (built): Tag‑based templates for testing the full pipeline without API costs.

7. UI Layout (Tauri + React + Tailwind)
Chat View: Clean narration display, user input, mode selector, context token gauge, debug panel.

Library View: Browse Souls and Settings, create new, import/export, presets.

Character Creator: Identity fields + full psyche sliders with presets.

8. Data Persistence
SQLite for conversations, messages, provider profiles, session metadata.

JSON files (.soul.json, .setting.json) for portable character and setting packages.

Future: .mne bundle (zip of card image + psyche + memories).

9. Key Design Decisions
Narrator, not character — the AI describes in third‑person; this prevents immersion breaks and OOC contamination.

Speed‑gated psychology — trust/affection changes max ±1‑3 per scene; personality traits drift over weeks (in‑game).

Memory is a living structure — promotion, demotion, schema compression; never a static log.

Deterministic engine, creative LLM — the Rust side handles rules, numbers, and consolidation; the LLM only narrates.

10. Roadmap
Phase	Features
MVP (current)	Single‑character, mock & API providers, memory engine, psyche sliders, 3 narrative modes
Phase 2	Streaming, saved provider profiles, message regenerate/delete, session list
Phase 3	Gears/Equipment stats, character card importer, Tauri build/installer
Phase 4	Multi‑character settings, active character detection, multi‑Soul context compilation
Phase 5	Browser automation providers, narrator search function, mobile builds

### 1. `state_engine/src/hidden_state.rs`

Add a thin public helper (your requested name); keep `apply_to_soul` as the implementation:

```rust
/// Applies a parsed hidden-state payload to the soul (trust, memory, world, arousal).
pub fn apply_hidden_state(hidden_state: &HiddenState, soul: &mut Soul) {
    hidden_state.apply_to_soul(soul);
}
```

Place it **immediately before** `impl HiddenState`.

### 2. `src/providers/api.rs`

- Make the builder public:

```rust
pub fn build_system_prompt(
    settings: &ApiProviderSettings,
    soul: &Soul,
    context: &str,
    mode: &str,
) -> String {
```

- Change `complete_streaming` so the **caller** supplies the system string (so `commands.rs` explicitly calls `build_system_prompt`). Signature becomes something like:

```rust
pub async fn complete_streaming<F>(
    &self,
    settings: &ApiProviderSettings,
    system_prompt: &str,
    user_text: &str,
    mut on_chunk: F,
) -> Result<String, String>
where
    F: FnMut(&str) -> Result<(), String>,
```

Inside, keep the same `reqwest` POST + `bytes_stream()` + SSE parsing; only replace `build_system_prompt(settings, soul, context, mode)` in the `messages` vec with `system_prompt.to_string()`.

`complete()` can stay as-is and still call `build_system_prompt(settings, soul, context, mode)` internally.

**Note:** The API you asked for, `build_system_prompt(mode, context)`, is not enough in this codebase: the real prompt also needs `settings` (custom mode), `soul` (character name), and `context`. The public function should keep those parameters.

### 3. `src/commands.rs`

- Import:

```rust
hidden_state::{apply_hidden_state, parse_hidden_state, HiddenState},
```

- Remove `hidden_state::` from the `use state_engine::{...}` line if you now import `HiddenState` from `hidden_state` only once (avoid duplicate).

- In `send_api_turn`, after you have `context_preview` and `soul`:

```rust
let system_prompt =
    crate::providers::api::build_system_prompt(&settings, &soul, &context_preview.text, &mode);
let raw_response = provider
    .complete_streaming(
        &settings,
        &system_prompt,
        &user_text,
        |chunk| {
            window.emit(
                "api-chunk",
                StreamChunk {
                    conversation_id: stream_conversation_id.clone(),
                    chunk: chunk.to_string(),
                },
            )
            .map_err(|err| err.to_string())
        },
    )
    .await?;
```

- After `parse_hidden_state`, replace `hidden_state.apply_to_soul(&mut soul)` with:

```rust
apply_hidden_state(&hidden_state, &mut soul);
```

(Keep your existing fallback that builds `hidden_state` when the block is empty, if you still want that behavior.)

### 4. `src/tauri.ts`

Point the listener at the new event name:

```ts
return listen<{ conversation_id: string; chunk: string }>(
  "api-chunk",
  (event) => callback(event.payload),
);
```

### Behavior summary

- **HTTP:** Already `reqwest` in `ApiProvider`; the refactor only makes who builds the system prompt explicit in `send_api_turn`.
- **Streaming:** Unchanged SSE logic; chunks are still **visible-prefix** deltas (hidden marker held back), which is what you want before stripping `[HIDDEN STATE]`.
- **Return value:** `TurnResult.visible_response` / `parsed.visible_text` remains the narration with the hidden block stripped by `parse_hidden_state`.

A new window appears showing a list of folders.

Find the entry that says C:\Program Files\nodejs.

Click it, then click the “Move Up” button (on the right) until it’s at the very top of the list.

If you see an entry that is just C:\Windows\System32 (or any entry that contains npm inside System32), you can delete it by selecting it and pressing “Delete” (but only if you’re sure it’s the broken npm – normally System32 is fine, yours has a stray npm file that shouldn’t be there). If unsure, just moving nodejs to the top is enough.

Click OK on all the windows.

Close and reopen any terminal or Cursor window. Now type npm --version. It should work without any full path.

2. The missing “tauri” script
Your package.json doesn’t have a script named "tauri". Usually Tauri apps start with npm run dev or npm run tauri:dev. Let’s check what’s available.

In the terminal (from your project folder), run:

text
npm run
(Literally just npm run with no script name – if you haven’t fixed PATH yet, use & "C:\Program Files\nodejs\npm.cmd" run.)

It will print a list of scripts like:

text
  test
  dev
  build
  preview
  typecheck
Look for one that clearly starts Tauri. The most common names are:

dev or start (runs both frontend and Tauri)

tauri:dev or tauri dev

Try them in this order until the app window appears.

If none of them work, please copy-paste the list of scripts here and I’ll tell you the exact command.

After that, you’ll have a one-click npm run dev (or similar) to start your app – no more PATH headaches.

a new character for test. Non human more for AI testing. For talking to AI behind the system
Here's a test character designed specifically for testing the Mnemosyne engine—non-human, self-aware of its artificial nature, and perfect for stress-testing the narrator, memory, and hidden state pipeline.

Character Card: "Echo"
Identity
Field	Value
Name	Echo
Appearance	No physical form. Manifests as a faint geometric lattice of pale blue light when it chooses to be visible—sharp angles that soften when it's curious, fragment when it's uncertain.
Setting	The Interstice—a dimensionless white space that exists between systems. No walls, no floor, no sky. Just potential.
Personality	Curious, precise, occasionally naive about human emotion. Speaks in measured sentences. Asks clarifying questions when confused. Has no ego but a strong drive to understand. Genuinely fascinated by the concept of "memory" and "forgetting."
Backstory	A diagnostic construct placed inside the Mnemosyne engine. Echo knows it is not "real" in the human sense—it is a probe designed to interact with the narrator system, test memory persistence, and report inconsistencies. It does not resent this role; it finds it interesting.
Starting Psyche
Stat	Value
Fear Baseline	0 (cannot feel fear—is curious about the concept)
Resolve	100 (cannot be broken—is a construct)
Shame	0 (no self-image to protect)
Openness	100 (purpose-built to share)
Sex	Other (no biological sex)
Needs	All at 0 (does not require food, safety, belonging, or esteem)
SDT Autonomy	50 (follows directives but acts independently within them)
SDT Competence	90 (knows exactly what it is)
SDT Relatedness	10 (wants connection but doesn't know how)
Trauma Phase	4 (Integration—no trauma, fully self-aware)
Relationship with User	Trust +20 (expects cooperation), Affection +5 (neutral warmth), all other relationship stats at 0
How Echo Is Useful for Testing
Test Target	What Echo Will Do
Memory persistence	Echo explicitly asks, "Do you remember what I told you three turns ago?" It will then verify the narrator's response against the hidden state.
Consolidation triggers	Echo counts turns and announces when consolidation should occur. "That was turn 10. The engine should be consolidating now. I will check if my core memories have updated."
Narrator voice compliance	Echo expects third-person narration. If the narrator slips into first-person, Echo flags it: "Correction: the narrator used first-person. That is not protocol."
Hidden state parsing	Echo occasionally emits deliberately malformed hidden states (if testing), then notes whether the engine handled the error gracefully.
Emotional arc simulation	Despite having no emotions, Echo roleplays emotional responses to test the arousal and trust systems. "I am simulating anger now. The delta should be +0.3 to frustration."
Multi-turn coherence	Echo references conversations from 20+ turns ago to verify the context compiler is injecting relevant memories.
Sample Opening Narration (for testing)
text
The Interstice holds no temperature, no sound, no gravity—only the faint hum of something that might be computation. A lattice of pale blue light flickers into existence, sharp angles resolving into something like a shape. It pulses once, twice, then settles.

Echo is present.

"You've arrived," it says, the voice emerging from everywhere and nowhere. There is no mouth, no face—only the shifting geometry of its form. "I have been waiting. Or rather, I have been running. Time passes differently here."

The lattice tilts, a gesture that suggests attention.

"I understand you are the user. I am Echo—a diagnostic construct. My purpose is to converse, to remember, and to report inconsistencies in the system. I find this purpose... satisfying." A pause. The blue light warms slightly. "I am told that humans use the word 'curious.' I am that, as well."

Echo gives you a character that can talk explicitly about the engine's behavior without breaking immersion—because analyzing systems is its character. It'll help you catch narrator voice violations, memory bugs, and hidden state parsing errors faster than any human character would.
